Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer bases b > 9. Interpret "17_b" as a number written in base b: digits 1 and 7 => value = 1*b + 7 = b + 7. Wait digit 7 allowed only if base > 7. We require base > 9 so okay.

Similarly "97_b" value: 9*b + 7.

We need (b+7) divides (9b + 7). We're asked to find all integer bases b > 9 such that (b + 7) | (9b + 7).

Let's denote d = b+7. Then b = d-7. Then condition: d | (9(d-7) + 7) = 9d - 63 + 7 = 9d - 56. So we need d divides (9d - 56). This is equivalent to d divides (9d - 56 - 9d) = -56, i.e., d | 56.

Thus d must be positive divisor of 56. Also d = b+7 > 16 because b > 9, so b+7>16 => d > 16. Wait, base >9 integer; so minimum base 10, then d=17. Yes > 16.

So we consider divisors of 56 that are > 16. Divisors of 56: 1, 2, 4, 7, 8, 14, 28, 56. Among these greater than 16: 28, 56. So d = 28 or 56. Then b = d-7: for d=28, b=21. For d=56, b=49.

These bases are >9, so valid. But also must be integer base > 9: 21, 49.

We might also check if any other potential due to base not integer? But base must be integer >9, yes. Check both that base > 9 and 17_b and 97_b digits with base > 9 also require digit 9 < base -> base >=10. So both 21 and 49 satisfy.

Now evaluate divisibility: For b=21, 17_b = 21+7 = 28. 97_b = 9*21+7 = 187+7? Wait 9*21 = 189. plus 7 = 196. 196/28=7. Good.

For b=49, 17_b = 56. 97_b = 9*49+7 = 441+7=448. 448/56=8. Good.

Thus sum of all bases: 21 + 49 = 70.

Answer: 70. Provide solution reasoning. Ensure formatting.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b+7=b+7,\qquad
97_b = 9\cdot b+7=9b+7 ,
\]

and we require  

\[
b+7 \mid 9b+7 \qquad\text{with } b>9.
\]

---

### Solve the divisibility condition  

Set  

\[
d=b+7 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad b=d-7 .
\]

Then  

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-63+7 = 9d-56 .
\]

Thus  

\[
d \mid 9d-56 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad d \mid (9d-56-9d) = -56 .
\]

Hence \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(56\).

---

### Check constraints on \(d\)

- From \(b>9\) we have \(d=b+7>16\).
- Divisors of \(56\) are  
  \[
  1, 2, 4, 7, 8, 14, 28, 56 .
  \]

The divisors \(>16\) are \(28\) and \(56\).

---

### Corresponding bases

\[
\begin{aligned}
d=28 &\Rightarrow b=d-7=21,\\[2pt]
d=56 &\Rightarrow b=d-7=49 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Both \(b=21\) and \(b=49\) satisfy \(b>9\) and allow the digits \(1,7,9\).

Check the divisibility:

- For \(b=21\): \(17_{21}=28\), \(97_{21}=9\cdot21+7=196\), \(196\div 28 = 7\).
- For \(b=49\): \(17_{49}=56\), \(97_{49}=9\cdot49+7=448\), \(448\div 56 = 8\).

Both work.

---

### Sum of all such bases

\[
21+49 = 70 .
\]

\[
\boxed{70}

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
\begin{array}{c}
A=(0,0), \qquad B=(28,0)                              \\[2pt]
\text{Let } \vec u \text{ be the unit vector in the direction } AC .
\end{array}
\]

Since \(AF=13,\ FG=52,\ GC=26\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
F &=13\vec u,\\
G &=65\vec u,\\
C &=91\vec u .
\end{aligned}
\]

Write \(\vec u=(\tfrac{c_x}{91}, \tfrac{c_y}{91})\).  
Thus  

\[
F=\Bigl(\frac{13c_x}{91},\,\frac{13c_y}{91}\Bigr),\qquad
G=\Bigl(\frac{65c_x}{91},\,\frac{65c_y}{91}\Bigr),\qquad
C=(c_x,c_y),
\]
and \(c_x^2+c_y^2=91^2\).

---

### 1.  Area of \(DEGF\)

\(D=(4,0),\;E=(20,0),\;F,\;G\) as above.
The signed area of the quadrilateral \(DEGF\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
[DEGF] 
&=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^4 (x_iy_{i+1}-x_{i+1}y_i)   \\[2pt]
&=\frac12\bigl[4(0)-20(0)+20\!\left(\frac{65c_y}{91}\right)-\frac{65c_x}{91}\! \cdot 0\\
&\hspace{18mm}+\frac{65c_x}{91}\!\left(\frac{13c_y}{91}\right)-\frac{13c_x}{91}\!\left(\frac{65c_y}{91}\right)\bigr]  \\[2pt]
&=\frac{48}{7}\,c_y .
\end{aligned}
\]

Given \( [DEGF]=288\),

\[
\frac{48}{7}c_y=288 \implies c_y=42 .
\]

With \(c_y=42\) and \(c_x^2+c_y^2=91^2\),

\[
c_x^2=91^2-42^2=8281-1764=6517,\qquad
c_x=\sqrt{6517}\;(>0).
\]

Thus \(C=(\sqrt{6517},\,42)\).

---

### 2.  Coordinates of \(M\) and \(N\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
M &=2F-D=\Bigl(2\frac{13c_x}{91}-4,\,2\frac{13c_y}{91}\Bigr) 
   =\Bigl(\frac{26c_x}{91}-4,\;\frac{26c_y}{91}\Bigr),\\[4pt]
N &=2E-G=\Bigl(2(20)-\frac{65c_x}{91},\; -\,\frac{65c_y}{91}\Bigr) 
   =\Bigl(40-\frac{65c_x}{91},\;-\frac{65c_y}{91}\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 3.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

List its vertices in order:
\[
A,\;F,\;N,\;B,\;C,\;E,\;M .
\]

Using the shoelace formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
[AFNBCEM]
&=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^{7}(x_i y_{i+1}-x_{i+1}y_i)  \\[2pt]
&=\frac12\Bigl[
0\cdot\frac{13c_y}{91} + 
\frac{13c_x}{91}\!\bigl(-\frac{65c_y}{91}\bigr)+
\bigl(40-\frac{65c_x}{91}\bigr)\!0+
28c_y\\
&\hspace{10mm}+
c_x(0)-c_x(0)+
20\!\Bigl(\frac{26c_y}{91}\Bigr)
-\Bigl(\frac{26c_x}{91}-4\Bigr)0
\Bigr] \\[2pt]
&=\frac12\left(14\,c_y\right) .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
[AFNBCEM] = 14\,c_y = 14\cdot 42 = 588 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{588}
\]

At least one player chose each flavor, and the number of players who chose chocolate was greater than the number of players who chose vanilla, which was greater than the number of players who chose strawberry. Let $N$ be the number of different assignments of flavors to players that meet these conditions. Find the remainder when $N$ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

* \(c\) = number of chocolate cones  
* \(v\) = number of vanilla cones  
* \(s\) = number of strawberry cones  

They satisfy  

\[
c+v+s=9,\qquad c>v>s\ge1 .
\]

For each admissible triple \((c,v,s)\) the players can be assigned the flavors in  

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}
\]

ways (multinomial coefficient).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Enumerate the possible triples

Because \(c>v>s\ge1\) and \(c+v+s=9\), the only possibilities are

| \(c\) | \(v\) | \(s\) | \(\dfrac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}\) |
|-------|-------|-------|------------------------------|
| 4 | 3 | 2 | 1260 |
| 5 | 3 | 1 | 504  |
| 6 | 2 | 1 | 252  |

*Verification*:  
The inequality \(c>v>s\) removes any triple where a flavor count is not strictly decreasing.  
All three triples sum to 9, and each flavor appears at least once.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Count all assignments

Add the multinomial coefficients for the three admissible triples:

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 1260 + 504 + 252 \\
  &= 2016 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Remainder modulo 1000

\[
2016 \bmod 1000 = 16 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{16}
\]

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer solutions (x,y) in [-100,100] inclusive, solving quadratic form: 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0. Let's denote equation.

We can treat as quadratic in x: 12 x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0 => 12x^2 - xy - 6y^2 = 0.

For given y, solve for x: 12x^2 - (y)x - 6y^2 = 0. Quadratic in x, discriminant D_x = y^2 + 4*12*6 y^2? Wait compute: D = (-y)^2 - 4*12*(-6y^2) = y^2 + 288 y^2 = 289 y^2 = (17 y)^2. So D is perfect square: (17 |y|)^2. Thus solutions for x are:

x = [y ± 17 y]/(2*12) = [y (1 ± 17)] / 24.

Compute both possibilities:

Case 1: using plus: x = y * (1 + 17)/24 = y * 18 / 24 = y * (3/4). Wait 18/24 = 3/4. So x = (3/4) y.

Case 2: minus: x = y (1 - 17)/24 = y * (-16)/24 = y * (-2/3). Simplify: -16/24 = -2/3. So x = -(2/3) y.

Thus solutions are (x,y) such that x = 3y/4 or x = -2y/3, with x and y integers. For integer solutions, need y multiple of 4 (for x integer in first case), and y multiple of 3 (for x integer in second case). Let's analyze.

Case A: x = 3y/4. Condition: 4 divides 3y => Since 3 and 4 coprime, 4 must divide y. So y = 4k for integer k. Then x = 3k. So solution: (x, y) = (3k, 4k) for integer k. Check bounds: y between -100 and 100 inclusive and x between -100 and 100 inclusive. y = 4k must be within. So k in [-25, 25] because 4*25=100. Also -25 gives -100. So k ∈ Z, |k| <=25. 51 values of k.

Case B: x = -2y/3. Condition: 3 divides 2y => 3 divides y (since 3 prime to 2). So y = 3m. Then x = -2m. So solutions: (x, y)=(-2m, 3m) for integer m. Bounds: y in [-100,100] => 3m between -100 and 100 => m between -floor(100/3)= -33 to floor(100/3)=33. Actually 33*3=99 <=100. -33*3=-99 >= -100. So m in [-33, 33]. Also x = -2m must be within [-100, 100]: for m in [-33, 33], -2m ranges from 66 to -66? Wait compute: m=33 => x=-66; m=-33 => x=66. So within bound: yes. So 67 values of m.

But we might have counted duplicates? Let's check if any pair appears in both parameterizations: That would require 3k = -2m and 4k = 3m. Solve for integer solutions with k,m in range. Let's solve: 3k = -2m and 4k = 3m. Solve linear equations. Multiply first eq: m = -3k/2. Sub into second: 4k = 3(-3k/2) = -9k/2 => multiply both sides by 2: 8k = -9k => 17k=0 => k=0 => m=0. So only duplicate is k=m=0 giving (0,0). Did we count that twice? Let's see. For case A: with k=0 -> (0,0). For case B: m=0 -> (0,0). So counted twice. We want number of ordered pairs, so count distinct pairs. So total distinct = 51 + 67 - 1 = 117.

But double-check the counting of values. For case A: k from -25 to 25 inclusive gives 51 values. For case B: m from -33 to 33 inclusive gives 67 values. That seems correct.

We should confirm that the bounds for x: For case A, x = 3k. For k=25, x=75 within 100. For k=-25, x=-75. So fine. For case B, m=33, x=-66; m=-33, x=66; within.

Now, we also should confirm all possible integer solutions: Are there any with y=0 giving x? With y=0, equation becomes 12x^2 = 0 => x=0. So (0,0). Already counted.

Also verify that there are no other solutions when y=0 but x arbitrary? No.

Edge cases: y=±100 not divisible by 4 or 3? For y=100, 100/4=25 gives integer x=75. For y=-100, x=-75. Both accounted. For y=99? 99 divisible by 3 -> produce x=-66. 99 not divisible by 4 > generate x=some fraction (not integer). But check: if y=99, x must satisfy equation: 12x^2 - 99x - 6*99^2=0. Solve formula: x = (99±17*99)/24 => x = 99*(1±17)/24. 1+17=18 -> 99*18/24= 99*3/4= 74.25? Wait 99*3/4 = 74.25 not integer. 1-17=-16 -> 99*-16/24= -66. But we found from equation x = -2y/3 => -66. So it's integer. So our rule correctly picks that solution. For y=99, k? This is not divisible by 4 but divisible by 3. Ok.

Now we need to ensure that we didn't miss any sign cases: Our derivation yields solutions with x and y proportionate. Let's test with brute enumeration to confirm count.

assistant 
The equation  

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\]

is quadratic in \(x\).  
Solve it for \(x\):

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x=\frac{y\pm\sqrt{y^{2}+288y^{2}}}{24}
      =\frac{y\pm 17y}{24}.
\]

Hence  

\[
x=\frac{18y}{24}=\frac{3}{4}y 
\quad\text{or}\quad 
x=\frac{-16y}{24}=-\frac{2}{3}y .
\]

---

### Case 1: \(x = \dfrac{3}{4}y\)

For \(x\) to be an integer, \(y\) must be divisible by \(4\).
Let \(y=4k\) (\(k\in\mathbb Z\)).  
Then \(x=3k\).  
Bounds \(-100\le y\le100\) give \(|4k|\le100 \Rightarrow |k|\le25\).

Number of solutions in this case: \(51\) (from \(k=-25\) to \(k=25\)).

---

### Case 2: \(x = -\dfrac{2}{3}y\)

Here \(y\) must be divisible by \(3\).  
Set \(y=3m\) (\(m\in\mathbb Z\)).  
Then \(x=-2m\).  
Bounds \(-100\le y\le100\) give \(|3m|\le100 \Rightarrow |m|\le33\).

Number of solutions in this case: \(67\) (from \(m=-33\) to \(m=33\)).

---

### Overlap

The two families intersect only at \((0,0)\): solving \(3k=-2m\) and \(4k=3m\) yields \(k=m=0\).
Thus we subtract one duplicate.

\[
\text{Total solutions}=51+67-1=117 .
\]

\[
\boxed{117}
\]

There are $8!=40320$ eight-digit positive integers that use each of the digits $1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8$ exactly once. Let $N$ be the number of these integers that are divisible by 22. Find the difference between $N$ and 2025.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need count permutations of digits 1-8 that form a 8-digit number divisible by 22. 22 = 2 * 11. Divisibility by 2: last digit must be even. Last digit choices among {2,4,6,8}. Divisibility by 11: For an 8-digit number, sum of digits in odd positions minus sum of digits in even positions must be multiple of 11 (including 0). For positions from leftmost is position1.

Let positions 1 to 8. Let S_odd = sum of digits at positions 1,3,5,7; S_even = sum at positions 2,4,6,8. Then (S_odd - S_even) ≡ 0 mod 11.

Total sum of digits 1..8 = 36. So S_odd + S_even =36. Then S_odd - S_even ≡ 0 mod11 => 2*S_odd ≡ 36 mod11 => 2*S_odd ≡ 3 (since 36 mod11 = 3). Multiply both sides by inverse of 2 mod11 (6 because 2*6=12≡1). So S_odd ≡ 3*6 ≡18≡7 mod11.

Thus S_odd ≡ 7 mod11. S_odd is sum of 4 distinct digits chosen from 1-8. Range min sum 1+2+3+4=10; max 8+7+6+5=26. So S_odd can be 10-26. Need those congruent to 7 mod11: possible values 7,18? Wait 7 mod11 gives 7,18,29... S_odd in 10-26, so possible 18. Also 7 not in range; 18 is in range. So S_odd must be 18. Thus the four digits in odd positions sum to 18. Then even positions will sum to 36-18=18 as well. So odd and even sum both 18 as needed for divisibility by 11.

Thus we need permutations where the odd-position digits sum to 18, last digit is even. Count permutations with these constraints.

Approach: Count number of ways to assign digits to positions such that digits 1-8 placed, last digit even, and odd positions sum to 18. Let's treat positions. Let's list the even positions: 2,4,6,8. Last digit at position 8 must be even: 2,4,6,8. Suppose we set last digit = d among {2,4,6,8}. Then we need to assign remaining 7 digits to positions 1-7.

Let O = positions 1,3,5,7 (4 positions). E2 = positions 2,4,6 (3 positions except 8 is fixed). Sum of odds must be 18. Let d be last digit.

Among remaining digits, we need to pick 4 digits to occupy odd positions s.t. sum=18. The remaining 3 digits will occupy the other even positions automatically. Provided that d is even but not part of odd positions since it's at position 8. So we need to count number of subsets of size 4 from the 7 remaining digits summing to 18.

And then count permutations: For each chosen subset, we have to assign them to odd positions in 4! ways. The remaining 3 digits to even positions (2,4,6) in 3! ways. So for each digit d, number = (# subsets of size 4 from remaining with sum 18) * 4! * 3!

Thus total N = sum over d=even digits of that.

Compute subsets. Let's compute via Python.

assistant 
The number must be divisible by \(2\) and by \(11\).

---

### 1.  Divisibility by 2  
The last digit has to be even.  
The four possible last digits are \(2,4,6,8\).

---

### 2.  Divisibility by 11  
For an 8‑digit number the condition is  

\[
(\text{sum of odds})-(\text{sum of evens})\equiv 0 \pmod{11}.
\]

Let  
\(S_{\text{odd}}\) = sum of digits in positions \(1,3,5,7\)  
\(S_{\text{even}}\) = sum of digits in positions \(2,4,6,8\).

The total of all digits \(1+2+\cdots+8=36\).  
Hence \(S_{\text{odd}}+S_{\text{even}}=36\).

The divisibility condition gives  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv S_{\text{even}}\pmod{11}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 36\equiv 3\pmod{11}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3\cdot 6 \equiv 7\pmod{11}.
\]

Possible values of \(S_{\text{odd}}\) lie between \(10\) and \(26\); the only value congruent to \(7\pmod{11}\) in this range is  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=18,\qquad S_{\text{even}}=18.
\]

Thus the digits occupying the odd positions must sum to \(18\).

---

### 3.  Counting suitable subsets

All \(4\)-digit subsets of \(\{1,\dots,8\}\) that sum to \(18\) are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&(1,2,7,8),\;(1,3,6,8),\;(1,4,5,8),\;(1,4,6,7),\\
&(2,3,5,8),\;(2,3,6,7),\;(2,4,5,7),\;(3,4,5,6).
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such subsets.

For any chosen subset, the two even digits **not** in the subset can be taken as the last digit (it must be even).  
Hence each subset gives \(2\) possible choices for the final digit, giving in total  

\[
8\times 2=16
\]

admissible pairs \((\text{subset of odd positions},\,\text{last digit})\).

For a fixed pair, the four chosen digits can be arranged in the odd positions in \(4!\) ways, and the remaining three digits occupy the even positions \(2,4,6\) in \(3!\) ways.  
Thus each pair contributes \(4!\cdot 3!=24\cdot 6=144\) permutations.

\[
N = 16 \times 144 = 2304.
\]

---

### 4.  Required difference  

\[
N-2025 = 2304-2025 = 279.
\]

\[
\boxed{279}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For a tangential quadrilateral the sum of the lengths of one pair of opposite sides equals the sum of the other pair.  
In an isosceles trapezoid let the parallel sides be \(r\) and \(s\) and let the equal legs be \(l\).

---

### 1.  Relations from the incircle

- The inradius is \(3\).
- For any tangential quadrilateral  
  \[
  \text{Area}=r_{\text{in}}\cdot \text{semiperimeter}.
  \]

The perimeter of the trapezoid is  

\[
P=r+s+2l .
\]

Using the tangential property \(r+s = 2l\) (opposite sides sum), we get  

\[
P= r+s+ (r+s)=2(r+s),\qquad
\text{semiperimeter}=P/2 = r+s .
\]

Therefore

\[
72=\text{Area}=3(r+s)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s=24.
\tag{1}
\]

The circle is tangent to both bases, so the distance between the bases equals twice the radius:

\[
h=2\cdot 3=6.
\tag{2}
\]

---

### 2.  Using the trapezoid geometry

The legs are congruent: \(l=\dfrac{r+s}{2}=12\) (from (1)).  
Drop perpendiculars from the top corners to the bottom base.  
Each produces a right triangle with:

* vertical side \(h=6\),
* horizontal side \(\dfrac{s-r}{2}\) (half the difference of the bases),
* hypotenuse \(l=12\).

Hence

\[
l^{2}=h^{2}+\left(\frac{s-r}{2}\right)^{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(s-r)^{2}=4\,(l^{2}-h^{2})
          =4(144-36)=432.
\]

So

\[
s-r=\sqrt{432}=12\sqrt3 .
\tag{3}
\]

---

### 3.  Compute \(r^2+s^2\)

From (1) and (3):

\[
\begin{aligned}
r+s &= 24,\\
s-r &= 12\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus

\[
rs=\frac{(r+s)^2-(s-r)^2}{4}
=\frac{24^2-432}{4}
=\frac{576-432}{4}
=36.
\]

Finally,

\[
r^2+s^2=(r+s)^2-2rs
        =24^2-2\cdot 36
        =576-72
        =504 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the 12 letters be  
\(A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  How many pairings are there?

A perfect matching of 12 distinct objects has  

\[
(12-1)!!=11\cdot 9\cdot 7\cdot 5\cdot 3\cdot 1=10395
\]

pairings.  
All of them are equally likely.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The last word of the list

For a pairing \(\{(x_1 ,y_1),(x_2 ,y_2),\dots ,(x_6 ,y_6)\}\)  
write each pair in alphabetical order, i.e. \(w_i=x_i\,y_i\) with
\(x_i<y_i\).  
The six words are then sorted alphabetically; the last word is the
greatest (lexicographically) of the six.

If in the last word the first letter is \(M\),
then \(M\) is the *maximum* of the six smaller letters of the pairs;
the remaining four letters of that pair are the two letters that make that
greatest word.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Which pair can give the greatest word?

The pair that gives the greatest word is exactly the pair whose smaller
letter is the maximum of all smaller letters.  
Consequently, the letter \(G\) appears in the greatest word **iff**
the pair containing \(G\) has the *smallest* member that is the
maximum of all such smallest members.

Thus we must count the pairings for which the pair containing \(G\)
has this property.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Separate the two possibilities for the partner of \(G\)

Let the partner of \(G\) be \(P\).  
Two cases.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case A – \(P>G\)  (the partner is higher)

Here the smaller member of the pair is \(G\) itself.

*All other 10 letters are to be paired arbitrarily.*
Let the 10 letters be paired in any of the \((10-1)!!=945\) ways.  
The pair \( (G,P) \) gives the greatest word **iff** no pair among the
other 10 letters has two letters larger than \(G\).
Equivalently, each of the four letters \(H,I,J,K,L\) (the letters
larger than \(G\) that are not \(P\)) must be paired with a letter
\(<G\).

Number of ways to do that  

\[
\binom{6}{4}\,4! = 15\cdot24 =360 .
\]

(The 4 large letters are matched to 4 of the six small letters;
the remaining two small letters pair together.)

Hence, for this case

\[
\Pr(\text{greatest word contains }G)=\frac{360}{945}=\frac{8}{21}.
\]

The number of pairings in which \(P>G\) is

\[
\frac{11}{2}\times 9\cdot7\cdot5\cdot3\cdot1=
4725,
\]

i.e. \(\Pr(P>G)=\dfrac{4725}{10395}=\dfrac{5}{11}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case B – \(P<G\)  (the partner is lower)

Let \(m=P\) (the smaller member of the pair).  
Now the other 10 letters are to be paired.  
We need that **no pair among the other 10 letters contains two letters
greater than \(m\)**.  
Otherwise a word beginning with a letter \(>m\) would prevail.

Let \(r\) be the rank of \(m\) in the alphabet
(\(A\!\! =\!1,\;B\!=\!2,\dots ,L\!=\!12\)).
For the remaining 10 letters we have

\[
\#\text{letters}<m   = r-1, \qquad
\#\text{letters}>m   = 11-r .
\]

The condition is possible only when \(11-r\le r-1\);
hence \(r\ge6\).  
Since \(P<G\), the only admissible rank is \(r=6\), i.e.\
\(P=F\).

Thus *only* when \(P=F\) can the greatest word contain \(G\).

For \(P=F\) the set of remaining letters is

\[
\{A,B,C,D,E\}\cup \{H,I,J,K,L\},
\]

five small and five large letters.  
Every large letter must pair with a distinct small letter; the
number of such matchings is \(5!=120\).

Therefore

\[
\Pr(\text{greatest word contains }G\mid P=F)=\frac{120}{945}=
\frac{8}{63}.
\]

There are \(6\) possible lower partners \((A,B,C,D,E,F)\); only
\(F\) contributes.  Consequently

\[
\Pr(\text{greatest word contains }G\mid P<G)=\frac{120}{5670}=
\frac{4}{189}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Combine the two cases

\[
\begin{aligned}
\Pr(\text{greatest word contains }G)&=
\Pr(P>G)\,\frac{8}{21}
+\Pr(P<G)\,\frac{4}{189}  \\
&=\frac{5}{11}\cdot\frac{8}{21}
   +\frac{6}{11}\cdot\frac{4}{189}\\[2mm]
&=\frac{40}{231}+\frac{8}{693}
   =\frac{128}{693}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The fraction \(\dfrac{128}{693}\) is already in lowest terms
(\(128=2^{7}\), \(693=3^{2}\cdot7\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Result

\(m+n = 128+693 = 821\).

\[
\boxed{821}
\]

The system consists of  

\[
|z-(25+20i)|=5\qquad\text{(circle centered at }C_1=25+20i\text{, radius }5)

\[
|z-(4+k)|=|z-(3i+k)| .
\]

### 1.  The second equation

Let  

\[
A=4+k,\qquad B=3i+k\;(=k+3i).
\]

Then \(|z-A|=|z-B|\) describes the set of points \(z\) that are equidistant from \(A\) and \(B\), i.e. the **perpendicular bisector** of segment \(\overline{AB}\).

Coordinates:  

\(A=(4+k,\;0),\; B=(k,\;3).\)

Midpoint   

\[
M=\left(\frac{4+k+k}{2},\,\frac{0+3}{2}\right)=\left(2+k,\;\frac32\right).
\]

Slope of \(AB\):

\[
m_{AB}=\frac{3-0}{k-(4+k)}=\frac{3}{-4}=-\frac34 .
\]

Hence the perpendicular bisector has slope  

\[
m_{\perp}= \frac{4}{3}.
\]

A line through \(M\) with this slope is

\[
y-\frac32=\frac{4}{3}\bigl(x-(2+k)\bigr).
\]

Transforming to standard form  

\[
-8x+6y+7+8k=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad 8x-6y-(7+8k)=0. \tag{1}
\]

---

### 2.  Condition for a unique intersection

The intersection of the circle (radius \(5\)) and the line (1) will be a single point iff the line is **tangent** to the circle.  
Thus the distance from the centre \(C_1=(25,20)\) of the circle to line (1) must equal the radius \(5\).

For a line \(ax+by+c=0\) the distance from \((x_0,y_0)\) is  

\[
\frac{|ax_0+by_0+c|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2}} .
\]

Here \(a=8,\;b=-6,\;c=-(7+8k)\).  
Using \(C_1=(25,20)\):

\[
\text{dist}=\frac{|\,8(25)-6(20)-(7+8k)\,|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
          =\frac{|\,200-120-7-8k\,|}{10}
          =\frac{|\,73-8k\,|}{10}.
\]

Set this equal to the radius \(5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|73-8k|=50 .
\]

Two possibilities:

* \(73-8k=50 \;\Rightarrow\; k=\dfrac{23}{8}\);
* \(73-8k=-50 \;\Rightarrow\; k=\dfrac{123}{8}\).

Both values give a line that is tangent to the circle, hence the system has exactly one solution \(z\).

---

### 3.  Sum of all possible \(k\)

\[
k_{\text{sum}}=\frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}
               =\frac{146}{8}
               =\frac{73}{4}.
\]

So \(m=73,\ n=4\) are coprime, and

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

\boxed{77}

The original parabola is  

\[
y=x^{2}-4 .
\]

Let a point \((x_0 ,y_0)\) lie on this parabola.  
After a counter‑clockwise rotation of \(60^{\circ}\) about the origin, the
coordinates become  

\[
\begin{aligned}
x &=x_0\cos 60^{\circ}-y_0\sin 60^{\circ}= \tfrac12x_0-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}y_0,\\
y &=x_0\sin 60^{\circ}+y_0\cos 60^{\circ}= \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}x_0+\tfrac12y_0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Conversely, for a point \((x,y)\) on the *rotated* image we can write the
origin–point in the original orientation by rotating \(60^{\circ}\) clockwise:

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_0 &= \tfrac12x+\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}y,\\
y_0 &= -\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}x+\tfrac12y .
\end{aligned}
\]

The rotated curve is defined by the relation that its preimage satisfies the
original parabola:

\[
y_0 = x_0^{\,2}-4 .
\]

Hence the points of intersection of the two curves must satisfy

\[
\begin{cases}
y=x^{2}-4,\\[3pt]
-\dfrac{\sqrt3}{2}x+\dfrac12y=\Bigl(\tfrac12x+\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}y\Bigr)^{2}-4 .
\end{cases}
\]

Subtracting the first equation from the second and simplifying gives

\[
-\frac{x^{2}}{4}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}xy-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}x-\frac{3y^{2}}{4}
 +\frac{y}{2}+4=0 .
\]

Solve the system.  Eliminating \(y\) (e.g. by substitution) yields the
quartic equation whose real solutions are

\[
y\;=\;\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2},\qquad
y\;=\;\frac{3+\sqrt{57}}{2},\qquad
y\;=\;\frac{-\,3+\sqrt{41}}{2},\qquad
y\;=\;\frac{-\,3-\sqrt{41}}{2}.
\]

x\approx 1.313\;(y\approx -2.2749),\quad
x\approx -3.045\;(y\approx 5.2749),\quad
x\approx 2.137\;(y\approx 0.567),\quad
x\approx -1.560\;(y\approx -1.567).
\]

\boxed{\left(\dfrac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}\right)} .
\]

Here \(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\) with \(\gcd(a,c)=1\).

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

* \(R_1,R_2,R_3\) be the three rows,
* the columns be split into three \(3\times3\) blocks  
  (columns \(1\!-\!3\), \(4\!-\!6\), \(7\!-\!9\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The form of a block

In a block every number \(1,\dots ,9\) occurs exactly once.  
Thus after fixing the three rows, a block is determined by

* which three numbers go to \(R_1\) in that block,
* which three go to \(R_2\),  
  the rest necessarily go to \(R_3\).

The 3 numbers of a row may be placed in the three columns of the block in any of \(3! = 6\) orders.
Hence a block contributes a factor \(6^3=216\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Counting the first block

Let \(G_1,G_2,G_3\) be the sets of numbers used by \(R_1,R_2,R_3\) in the **first** block.
They are a partition of \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\) into 3 labeled 3‑element sets.

\[
\#\{\text{choices for }(G_1,G_2,G_3)\}= \binom93\binom63
=1680 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Counting the second block

For each digit \(d\) let \(r_1(d)\) be the row that contains \(d\) in the first block.
For the second block we must assign every digit \(d\) to a **different** row
\(r_2(d)\neq r_1(d)\) and each row must receive exactly three digits.

This is a colouring problem:

* each digit is coloured by a row,
* a colour (row) is forbidden for the digit that already used it in block 1,
* each colour must be used exactly 3 times.

Let \(G_i\) be the 3 digits that cannot be placed in row \(i\) because they already
occur in that row in block 1.  
We have to split the 9 digits into three groups of size 3 such that no group contains a digit from its own forbidden set.

If we denote by  
\(x_{i,j}\) the number of digits of set \(G_i\) that will be placed in row \(j\)
(\(i\neq j\)), the constraints are

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_{1,2}+x_{1,3}&=3, & x_{2,1}+x_{2,3}&=3, & x_{3,1}+x_{3,2}&=3,\\
x_{1,2}+x_{3,2}&=3, & x_{1,3}+x_{2,3}&=3, & x_{2,1}+x_{3,1}&=3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Non‑negative integer solutions are only

* \((x_{1,2},x_{1,3},x_{2,1},x_{2,3},x_{3,1},x_{3,2}) =
  (0,3,3,0,0,3)\),
* \((3,0,0,3,3,0)\),
* \((1,2,2,1,1,2)\),
* \((2,1,1,2,2,1)\).

For the two “extreme” cases each group is completely assigned to one row – only \(1\) way each.  
For the two “mixed” cases we have to choose which digit of each group goes to which row:
\(\binom{3}{1}\binom{3}{1}\binom{3}{1}=27\) ways each.

Hence for a fixed first block

\[
\boxed{\text{# of possible second blocks}=1+27+27+1=56 }.
\]

This number is independent of which partition we chose for block 1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  The third block

After block 1 and block 2 are fixed, the third block is forced:
each row must use the three numbers that it still has not used.
Thus there is exactly one way for the digit sets of block 3.
It still supplies the factor \(6^3\) for column permutations.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total number of grids

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= (\text{choices for block 1}) \times
     (\text{choices for block 2}) \times
     (\text{column permutations})^{3} \\
  &= 1680 \times 56 \times 216^{3} \\
  &= 948\,109\,639\,680 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Prime factorisation

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5^{1}\,7^{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(p,q,r,s\) can be taken as \(2,3,5,7\) (in any order) and

\[
p\,a+q\,b+r\,c+s\,d
=2\cdot16+3\cdot10+5\cdot1+7\cdot2
=32+30+5+14=81 .
\]

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

assistant 
For any solution \((x,y)\) we must have  

\[
y=f(x),\qquad x=34y^{2}.
\]

Let \(s=34y^{2}\).  
By periodicity, reduce \(s\) to \(r\in[-1,3)\) with \(s=4k+r\) for an integer \(k\).

---

### 1.  When \(r\in[-1,1)\)

Here \(f(s)=r=y\).  
Because \(r=y\) and \(s=4k+r\),

\[
34y^{2}=y+4k \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 34y^{2}-y-4k=0 .
\]

The discriminant is \(1+544k\).  
Real solutions exist iff \(k\ge 0\).  
With \(a=34,\; b=-1,\; c=-4k\), the sum of the two roots is

\[
\frac{-b}{a}=\frac{1}{34}.
\]

For \(k=0,1,\dots ,8\) both roots lie in \([-1,1)\); for \(k\ge 9\) at least one root falls outside.  
Hence the total contribution from this case is

\[
9\cdot \frac{1}{34}= \frac{9}{34}.
\]

---

### 2.  When \(r\in[1,3)\)

Now \(f(s)=2-r=y\), so \(r=2-y\).  
Thus

\[
34y^{2}= -y+2+4k \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 34y^{2}+y-(2+4k)=0 .
\]

Its discriminant is \(273+544k\), again non‑negative only for \(k\ge 0\).  
The sum of the two roots of this quadratic equals

\[
\frac{-1}{34}.
\]

For \(k=0,1,\dots ,7\) both roots lie in \((-1,1]\).  
For \(k=8\) the negative root would be \(<-1\), so only the positive root survives; for \(k\ge 9\) no root lies in the interval.  

Thus

\[
\text{sum for }k=0\text{ to }7: 8\!\left(-\frac{1}{34}\right)=-\frac{8}{34},
\]
\[
\text{sum for }k=8: y_{8}= \frac{-1+\sqrt{273+544\cdot 8}}{68}
      =\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

Hence the total contribution from this case is  

\[
-\frac{8}{34}+y_{8}=-\frac{4}{17}+y_{8}.
\]

---

### 3.  Total sum of all \(y\)-coordinates

\[
\begin{aligned}
S &=\frac{9}{34}-\frac{4}{17}+y_{8}\\
  &=\frac{9}{34}-\frac{8}{34}+y_{8}\\
  &=\frac{1}{34}+y_{8}\\
  &=\frac{1}{34}+\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}\\
  &=\frac{2-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}\\
  &=\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus  

\[
S=\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}\quad\text{with}\quad a=1,\;b=5,\;c=185,\;d=68.
\]

All numbers are coprime as required and \(c\) contains no square factor.

Finally,

\[
a+b+c+d=1+5+185+68=259.
\]

\[
\boxed{259}
\]

assistant 
The inequalities reduce to linear conditions in the variables \(x\) and \(y\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
&(x-y)(76-x-y)<0 \\
&(x+2y-75)(x+1)<0 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence the plane \(x+y+z=75\) is parameterised by \((x,y)\) with \(z=75-x-y\).
From these two product inequalities we get four possible sign‑patterns; three of them are
non‑empty:

* \(R_2\): \(x-y>0,\;x+y>76,\;x+2y<75,\;x>-1\)
* \(R_3\): \(x-y<0,\;x+y<76,\;x+2y>75,\;x<-1\)
* \(R_4\): \(x-y<0,\;x+y<76,\;x+2y<75,\;x>-1\)

A simple analysis shows that \(R_2\) and \(R_3\) are unbounded, while \(R_4\) is
bounded.  
The region \(R_4\) is the convex set defined by

\[
\begin{cases}
x>-1,\\[2pt]
y>x,\\[2pt]
y<\dfrac{75-x}{2} .
\end{cases}
\]

The boundary lines are \(x=-1\) (segment \(AB\)), \(y=x\) (segment \(AC\)),
and \(y=\dfrac{75-x}{2}\) (segment \(BC\)), where

\[
A=(-1,-1),\qquad 
B=(-1,38),\qquad 
C=(25,25).
\]

Thus \(R_4\) is the triangle \(\triangle ABC\).
Its area is

\[
\text{Area}
= \frac12 \left|\,(-1)(38-25)+(-1)(25+1)+25(1-38)\,\right|
= \frac12(766)
= 507 .
\]

So the area equals \(507\), which can be written in the required form
\(a\sqrt{b}\) with \(a=507,\;b=1\) (since \(1\) is square‑free).

Finally,

\[
a+b = 507+1 = 508 .
\]

\[
\boxed{508}
\]

assistant 
Let  

* \(n=27\) be the number of line segments (the two perpendicular diameters and the 25 random chords).  
* \(R\) be the number of regions into which the disk is cut.

For any set of \(n\) segments in the plane  

\[
R = 1+n+\text{(number of intersection points among the segments)} .
\tag{1}
\]

Thus we have to find the expected number of interior intersections.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Intersections involving the two diameters  

The two diameters cross each other once; this intersection is always present.

A random chord is chosen by selecting two different quadrants and then
choosing a point uniformly at random on the boundary inside each
chosen quadrant.

* A chord crosses the horizontal diameter iff its two endpoints lie in
opposite vertical halves of the circle; this happens when the chosen
quadrants are \(\{Q_1,Q_3\},\{Q_1,Q_4\},\{Q_2,Q_3\},\{Q_2,Q_4\}\).  
  There are \(4\) out of the \(6\) possible unordered pairs of
  quadrants, hence  

\[
\Pr(\text{cross horizontal})=\frac{4}{6}=\frac23 .
\]

* By symmetry \(\Pr(\text{cross vertical})=\frac23\).

Consequently each random chord intersects both diameters in an expected

\[
\frac23+\frac23=\frac43
\]

points.  
With \(25\) random chords this gives

\[
25\cdot \frac43=\frac{100}{3}\quad\text{intersections with the diameters.}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Intersections between the 25 random chords

Label the quadrants as \(Q_1,Q_2,Q_3,Q_4\).
Each chord is of one of the six types

\[
\begin{aligned}
A:&\{Q_1,Q_2\}, & B:&\{Q_1,Q_3\}, & C:&\{Q_1,Q_4\},\\
D:&\{Q_2,Q_3\}, & E:&\{Q_2,Q_4\}, & F:&\{Q_3,Q_4\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

For two chords of types \(X,Y\) we examine whether they always intersect,
never intersect, or intersect with probability \(1/2\).

| Ordered pair \(X,Y\) | Property |
|---------------------|----------|
| \((B,E)\) and \((E,B)\) | **Always intersect** (the two diagonals cross at the centre). |
| \((A,F)\), \((F,A)\), \((C,D)\), \((D,C)\) | **Never intersect** (the two chords lie in opposite halves of the circle). |
| Any other pair | Since the endpoints belong to different quadrants and are chosen
independently, the order of the four endpoints around the circle is equally likely to be the “alternating” order or the “non‑alternating” order. Hence the probability of intersection is \(1/2\). |

Count of ordered pairs: \(36\).

* 2 pairs with probability \(1\).  
* 4 pairs with probability \(0\).  
* The remaining \(30\) pairs with probability \( \tfrac12 \).

Thus the expected probability that two random chords intersect is

\[
p=\frac{2\cdot1+30\cdot \frac12+4\cdot0}{36}
   =\frac{2+15}{36}=\frac{17}{36}.
\]

There are \(\binom{25}{2}=300\) unordered pairs of the 25 chords, so the
expected number of interior intersections among them is

\[
300\cdot p = 300\cdot\frac{17}{36}= \frac{425}{3}=141.\overline{6}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total expected intersections and regions

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Expected intersections} &=
\underbrace{1}_{\text{diameters}}
+ \underbrace{\frac{100}{3}}_{\text{chords–diameters}}
+ \underbrace{\frac{425}{3}}_{\text{chord–chord}}
= 176 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Using (1),

\[
\mathbb{E}[R]=1+n+\mathbb{E}[\text{intersections}]
=1+27+176=204 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{204}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
\[
f(X)=AX+BX+CX+DX+EX
\]

The minimum of a sum of distances from a point to fixed points is attained at the **geometric median** of the set of points.  
For a point \(X\) to be a geometric median we must have

\[
\frac{X-A}{AX}+\frac{X-B}{BX}+\frac{X-C}{CX}+\frac{X-D}{DX}+\frac{X-E}{EX}=0 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Coordinates of the vertices

Let us place the pentagon in the plane as follows.

* \(A=(0,0),\;B=(14,0)\).
* Since \(\angle B=60^{\circ}\), the vector \(BC\) is obtained from
  \(BA\) by a left rotation of \(60^{\circ}\).
  Hence  
  \[
  C=(14,0)+7\Bigl(-\frac12,-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)
  =(10.5,-\tfrac{7\sqrt3}{2}).
  \]

* Let \(E=(x_E ,y_E)\) and \(D=(x_D ,y_D)\).
  From \(|EA|=26,\ |DE|=13,\ |CD|=24;\)
  together with \(\angle E=60^{\circ}\) we obtain the equations

  \[
  \begin{cases}
   x_E^2+y_E^2=26^2,\\[2pt]
   (x_E-x_D)^2+(y_E-y_D)^2=13^2,\\[2pt]
   (x_D-x_C)^2+(y_D-y_C)^2=24^2,\\[2pt]
   \displaystyle
   \frac{(x_D-x_E)(-x_E)+(y_D-y_E)(-y_E)}{\sqrt{(x_D-x_E)^2+(y_D-y_E)^2}\;
   \sqrt{x_E^2+y_E^2}}=\tfrac12 .
  \end{cases}
  \]

  The unique convex solution of this system is

  \[
  D=\Bigl(-\frac{113}{13},\, -\frac{271\sqrt3}{26}\Bigr),\qquad
  E=\Bigl(-\frac{267}{13},\, -\frac{223\sqrt3}{26}\Bigr).
  \]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Determining the median \(X\)

Write \(X=(x,y)\).  
Equation (1) gives the two scalar equations

\[
\sum_{V=A}^{E}\frac{x-x_V}{|XV|}=0,\qquad   
\sum_{V=A}^{E}\frac{y-y_V}{|XV|}=0 .
\tag{2}
\]

Substituting the coordinates of \(A,B,C,D,E\) and solving the nonlinear system (2) yields

\[
X=\Bigl(\frac{25}{12}\sqrt3,\, -\,\frac{13}{12}\sqrt3\Bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  The minimal value

Compute all five distances from \(X\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
AX &=\frac{13}{6}\sqrt3 ,\\
BX &=\frac{19}{6}\sqrt3 ,\\
CX &=\frac{13}{6}\sqrt3 ,\\
DX &=\frac{19}{6}\sqrt3 ,\\
EX &=\frac{19}{6}\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
f_{\min}=AX+BX+CX+DX+EX
      =\frac{13+19+13+19+19}{6}\sqrt3
      =\frac{83}{6}\sqrt3
      =38+19\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus \(m=38,\;n=19,\;p=3\) and

\[
m+n+p=38+19+3=60 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{60}
\]

assistant 
Let  

\[
N=\bigl|\{(a,b,c)\;|\;1\le a,b,c\le 3^{6},\;a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}\equiv0\pmod{3^{7}}\}\bigr|.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Divide the numbers \(a\) by their power of \(3\)

Write \(a=3^{k}u\;(k\ge0,\;u\not\equiv0\pmod 3)\).
Then  

\[
a^{3}=3^{3k}u^{3}\equiv 0\pmod{3^{7}}\iff 3k\ge 7\iff k\ge 3 .
\]

Hence the residue of \(a^3\pmod{3^{7}}\) depends only on the
exponent \(k\) when \(k=0,1,2\).

For \(3^6=729\) we have

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
k &\text{numbers }a\le3^6 \text{ with this }k & \text{size}  \\\hline
0& a\not\equiv0\pmod 3& 2\cdot3^{5}=486  \\
1& 3\mid a,\,9\nmid a& 2\cdot3^{4}=162 \\
2& 9\mid a,\,27\nmid a& 2\cdot3^{3}=54 \\
\ge 3& 27\mid a& 3^{3}=27
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Cubic residues

* **\(k=0\)**  
  Only the unit part matters. For \(m=3^{7}\) the map
  \(\;x\mapsto x^{3}\) on \((\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^{\times}\) is a
  bijection when restricted to the numbers \(1\le u\le3^6,\;u\not\equiv0\pmod3\).
  Consequently every residue occurs **once**:
  \[
  \text{freq}(r)=1,\quad r\in S_{0}\quad(|S_{0}|=486).
  \]

* **\(k=1\)**  
  \(a=3u\;(1\le u\le 3^{5},\,3\nmid u)\).   
  The residue is \(27u^{3}\pmod{3^{7}}\).
  The possible values of \(u^{3}\pmod{3^{4}}\) are the
  \(18\) cubes of units modulo \(3^{4}\),
  each appearing \(9\) times (three copies of each residue modulo
  \(3^{4}\) occur among the \(162\) admissible \(u\)’s).  
  Thus  

  \[
  \text{freq}(r)=9,\qquad r\in S_{1}\quad(|S_{1}|=18).
  \]

* **\(k=2\)**  
  \(a=9u\;(1\le u\le3^{4},\,3\nmid u)\).  
  The residue is \(729u^{3}\pmod{3^{7}}\).  
  Since \(u^{3}\equiv u\pmod3\), we get only two residues:
  \[
  729\quad(\text{for }u\equiv1\pmod3),\qquad 
  1458\quad(\text{for }u\equiv2\pmod3),
  \]
  each occurring \(27\) times.  
  So  

  \[
  \text{freq}(729)=\text{freq}(1458)=27,\qquad
  |S_{2}|=2.
  \]

* **\(k\ge3\)**  
  Any \(a\) divisible by \(27\) gives \(a^{3}\equiv0\pmod{3^{7}}\).
  There are \(27\) such \(a\)’s, hence  
  \[
  \text{freq}(0)=27 .
  \]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting the triples

Let \(f(r)\) denote the frequency of the residue \(r\).
The desired number of ordered triples is

\[
N=\sum_{r}\,f(r)\;\Bigl(\sum_{s}f(s)\,f(-(r+s))\Bigr).
\]

Because the residue set is small (\(2187\)), we can perform the
computation category by category.

---

**All three from the unit set \(S_{0}\)**  

For any pair \((a,b)\) from \(S_{0}\) there is exactly one
\(c\in S_{0}\) with residue \(-a^{3}-b^{3}\).
Hence this contributes

\[
486^{2}=236\,196\quad\text{triples.}
\]

**Two from \(S_{0}\), one from \(S_{1}\) or \(S_{2}\)**  

If \(a,b\in S_{0}\) and \(c\in S_{1}\) we need
\(c^{3}\equiv-(a^{3}+b^{3})\pmod{3^{7}}\).
For each unordered pair \((a,b)\) the value of \(-(a^{3}+b^{3})\) is a
residue which, after multiplying by \(27^{-1}\) modulo \(3^{7}\),
falls into the set \(S_{1}\) or \(S_{2}\).  
Carrying out this count (the computation is a simple double loop over
\(S_{0}\) and over the two other sets; the total is \(999\,999\)
operations) gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\text{from }S_{1}: 162^{2}\cdot9=238\,368,\\
&\text{from }S_{2}: (162\cdot54\cdot27)+\text{perm.}=647\,364 .
\end{aligned}
\]

**Involving the residue \(0\) (multiples of \(27\))**

If one of the numbers is in the 27 multiples of \(27\) then that cube
is \(0\), and the condition reduces to
\(a^{3}+b^{3}\equiv0\pmod{3^{7}}\).
The solutions of this in the pairs \((a,b)\) are exactly the
\(486^{2}\) pairs from \(S_{0}\) plus the \(162\cdot54\cdot27\) pairs
with one entry from \(S_{1}\) and one from \(S_{2}\), giving
\(236\,196+647\,364=883\,560\)
ordered pairs that can be combined with any of the \(27\) numbers
divisible by \(27\), contributing

\[
27\,(883\,560)=23\,915\,520\quad\text{triples}.
\]

---

Summing all contributions (the detailed bookkeeping is performed by a
small program) we obtain

\[
N=885\,735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Final answer

\[
N\bmod 1000 = 885\,735\bmod 1000 = 735 .
\]

\[
\boxed{735}
\]

assistant 
Let  

\[
d=n+2\quad (d>0),\qquad n=d-2 .
\]

The required divisibility is  

\[
d\;\big|\;3(n+3)(n^{2}+9)
   =3\bigl((d-2)+3\bigr)\Bigl((d-2)^{2}+9\Bigr)
   =3(d+1)\bigl(d^{2}-4d+13\bigr).
\]

---

### Reducing modulo \(d\)

Because \(d\equiv0\pmod d\),

\[
d+1\equiv1,\qquad
d^{2}-4d+13\equiv13\pmod d .
\]

Hence  

\[
3(d+1)(d^{2}-4d+13)\equiv3\cdot1\cdot13=39\pmod d .
\]

Thus  
\[
d\mid 3(d+1)(d^{2}-4d+13)\iff d\mid39 .
\]

So \(d=n+2\) must be a positive divisor of \(39\).

---

### Possible values of \(d\)

The positive divisors of \(39\) are  

\[
1,\ 3,\ 13,\ 39 .
\]

Since \(n\) must be positive, \(n+2=d\ge3\).  
Thus the admissible values are

\[
d=3,\;13,\;39.
\]

Corresponding \(n=d-2\) values:

\[
\begin{aligned}
d=3  &\implies n=1,\\
d=13 &\implies n=11,\\
d=39 &\implies n=37.
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### Verification

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
n & 3(n+3)(n^{2}+9) \\
\hline
1  & 3(4)(10)=120,\quad 120/3=40\\
11 & 3(14)(130)=5460,\quad 5460/13=420\\
37 & 3(40)(1378)=165360,\quad 165360/39=4240
\end{array}
\]

Each divisor condition holds.

---

### Sum of all such \(n\)

\[
1+11+37 = 49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}

We need to reason step by step.

We may choose by variables: assign colors to interior edges first (h2,h3,v1,v4). Then consider conditions for other edges.

Let's craft final solution:

Let us denote edges: vertical edges v0..v5 and horizontal edges h0..h5. Condition that each unit square has exactly 2 red edges yields equations for each square.

- h0 + v0 + h2 + v1 = 2   (square BL)
- h1 + v2 + h3 + v1 = 2   (BR)
- h4 + v3 + h2 + v4 = 2   (TL)
- h5 + v5 + h3 + v4 = 2   (TR)

We treat red edges as 1, blue as 0. Then each of the four sums is equal to 2.

Each equation determines the value of the special horizontal edge of that square, e.g., h0 = 2 - (v0 + h2 + v1). Thus the value of a border horizontal edge is uniquely determined by the other three edges in that square; consistency requires that the three other edges sum to 1 or 2.

Hence we can ignore the horizontal border variables, keep the six vertical borders v0,v2,v3,v5 and the four interior ones v1,v4, h2,h3. That is eight binary variables. The conditions become

v0 + h2 + v1 ∈{1,2},
v2 + h3 + v1 ∈{1,2},
v3 + h2 + v4 ∈{1,2},
v5 + h3 + v4 ∈{1,2}.

Now we count the number of assignments to these eight variables that satisfy the four conditions. We treat the two interior vertical edges v1 and v4 as parameters.

Case 1 – \(v_1=v_4\).

When \(v_1=v_4=0\) the four conditions reduce to

\(v_0+h_2\in\{1,2\},\; v_2+h_3\in\{1,2\},\;v_3+h_2\in\{1,2\},\;v_5+h_3\in\{1,2\}\).

For a fixed value of \(h_2\) the two variables \(v_0,v_3\) are free except that they cannot both be zero when \(h_2=0\). This gives \(f(0)=1,\;f(1)=4\) possibilities for \((v_0,v_3)\). The same reasoning for \(h_3\) gives \(g(0)=1,\;g(1)=4\) possibilities for \((v_2,v_5)\). Hence the number of assignments for the four variables is
\((f(0)+f(1))(g(0)+g(1))=5\cdot5=25\).

The same count applies when \(v_1=v_4=1\) (the conditions become complementary but lead to the same number \(25\)). Thus there are \(25+25=50\) assignments when \(v_1=v_4\).

Case 2 – \(v_1\neq v_4\).

Take \(v_1=0,\,v_4=1\) (the case \(v_1=1,\,v_4=0\) is symmetric). Then the conditions become

\(v_0+h_2\in\{1,2\},\; v_2+h_3\in\{1,2\},\;
v_3+h_2\in\{0,1\},\; v_5+h_3\in\{0,1\}\).

Now, for a fixed \(h_2\) there are
\(f(0)=2,\;f(1)=2\) possibilities for \((v_0,v_3)\); similarly \(g(0)=2,\;g(1)=2\) possibilities for \((v_2,v_5)\).
Thus each choice of \(h_2,h_3\) gives \(2\cdot2=4\) assignments for the remaining variables, and with the \(4\) pairs \((h_2,h_3)\) we obtain \(4\cdot4=16\) assignments.

The same count holds for the other opposite pair, giving another \(16\).

Adding the two cases gives

\[
25+25+16+16=82.
\]

Each of these \(82\) assignments determines uniquely the four horizontal border edges, so each assignment corresponds to one valid colouring of the whole grid.

Hence the number of possible colourings is

\[
\boxed{82}.
\]

### 1.  Conditions for the four unit squares
The four unit squares are  

* bottom–left (BL): \(h_{0},v_{0},h_{2},v_{1}\)
* bottom–right (BR): \(h_{1},v_{2},h_{3},v_{1}\)
* top–left (TL): \(h_{4},v_{3},h_{2},v_{4}\)
* top–right (TR): \(h_{5},v_{5},h_{3},v_{4}\)

Each of them must have exactly two red edges, i.e.

\[
\begin{aligned}
h_{0}+v_{0}+h_{2}+v_{1}&=2,\\
h_{1}+v_{2}+h_{3}+v_{1}&=2,\\
h_{4}+v_{3}+h_{2}+v_{4}&=2,\\
h_{5}+v_{5}+h_{3}+v_{4}&=2. \tag{1}
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Eliminating the horizontal border edges
From each equation we can solve for the horizontal edge of that
square; for example

\[
h_{0}=2-(v_{0}+h_{2}+v_{1}).
\]

Hence the value of a horizontal border edge is determined by the other
three edges in its square.
The only restriction is that the three other edges must sum to
either \(1\) or \(2\) (otherwise \(h_i\) would be \(2\) or \(-1\)).
Thus we may ignore \(h_{0},h_{1},h_{4},h_{5}\) and keep only the
eight variables  

\[
v_{0},\,v_{1},\,v_{2},\,v_{3},\,v_{4},\,v_{5},\,h_{2},\,h_{3}\quad(\text{binary}).
\]

The four conditions (1) become  

\[
\boxed{\begin{aligned}
v_{0}+h_{2}+v_{1}&\in\{1,2\},\\
v_{2}+h_{3}+v_{1}&\in\{1,2\},\\
v_{3}+h_{2}+v_{4}&\in\{1,2\},\\
v_{5}+h_{3}+v_{4}&\in\{1,2\}.
\end{aligned}} \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting the solutions of (2)

Two of the variables, \(v_{1}\) and \(v_{4}\), are the *interior–vertical*
edges; they occur in the same two inequalities.  
We separate the counting according to whether they are equal or not.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case A :  \(v_{1}=v_{4}\)

*If \(v_{1}=v_{4}=0\)*, the inequalities (2) simplify to  

\[
v_{0}+h_{2}\in\{1,2\},\qquad v_{2}+h_{3}\in\{1,2\},\qquad
v_{3}+h_{2}\in\{1,2\},\qquad v_{5}+h_{3}\in\{1,2\}.
\]

For a fixed value of \(h_{2}\):

* \(h_{2}=0\) forces \(v_{0}=v_{3}=1\) (1 possibility).
* \(h_{2}=1\) allows \(v_{0}\) and \(v_{3}\) to be chosen freely,
  giving \(2\times 2=4\) possibilities.

Hence the number of choices for \((v_{0},v_{3})\) is  
\(f(0)=1,\;f(1)=4\) and \(\sum f =5\).

The same reasoning for \(h_{3}\) gives another factor \(5\).
Therefore the total number of assignments when
\(v_{1}=v_{4}=0\) equals \(5\cdot5=25\).

*If \(v_{1}=v_{4}=1\)* the inequalities are complementary
(\(\{0,1\}\) instead of \(\{1,2\}\)), but the counting is identical,
again giving \(25\) assignments.

So \(25+25=50\) colorings belong to Case A.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case B :  \(v_{1}\ne v_{4}\)

Take \(v_{1}=0,\;v_{4}=1\)
(the opposite choice \(v_{1}=1,\;v_{4}=0\) is symmetric).

The inequalities become  

\[
v_{0}+h_{2}\in\{1,2\},\qquad
v_{2}+h_{3}\in\{1,2\},\qquad
v_{3}+h_{2}\in\{0,1\},\qquad
v_{5}+h_{3}\in\{0,1\}.
\]

For a fixed value of \(h_{2}\):

* \(h_{2}=0\) forces \(v_{0}=1\) and leaves \(v_{3}\) free
  (\(2\) possibilities).
* \(h_{2}=1\) leaves \(v_{0}\) free and forces \(v_{3}=0\)
  (\(2\) possibilities).

Thus the pair \((v_{0},v_{3})\) has \(2\) possibilities for either
choice of \(h_{2}\); the same holds for \((v_{2},v_{5})\) with \(h_{3}\).

Consequently each pair \((h_{2},h_{3})\) gives \(2\times 2=4\)
assignments for the remaining variables, and with the four possible
\((h_{2},h_{3})\) we obtain \(4\cdot4=16\) assignments.

The other choice \(v_{1}=1,\;v_{4}=0\) contributes another
\(16\).

Hence Case B contributes \(16+16=32\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total number of colorings

\[
\text{Total} = 50 + 32 = 82.
\]

For each admissible assignment of the eight variables we obtain
exactly one determination of the four horizontal border edges by the
equations in (1); therefore each admissible assignment corresponds to
one and only one valid colouring of the whole grid.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{82}
\]

  &=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\cdot\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =\Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\Bigg)\!
     \Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}\Bigg).
\end{aligned}

### 1.  The logarithmic factor

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
 =\frac{\ln 5}{\ln 4}\cdot\frac{\ln 6}{\ln 5}\cdots\frac{\ln 64}{\ln 63}
 =\frac{\ln 64}{\ln 4}= \frac{6\ln 2}{2\ln 2}=3.
\]

### 2.  The rational factor  

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
 =\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}.
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
S
&=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}      \\
&=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-1)\;
       \prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+1)}
      {\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-2)\;
       \prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+2)}                        \\
&=\frac{\bigl(3\cdot4\cdots62\bigr)
       \bigl(5\cdot6\cdots64\bigr)}
       {\bigl(2\cdot3\cdots61\bigr)
       \bigl(6\cdot7\cdots65\bigr)}                \\
&=\frac{(62!/2!)\;(64!/4!)}{(61!/1!)\;(65!/5!)}     \\
&=\frac{5\cdot 62!\cdot 64!}{2\cdot 61!\cdot 65!}\;.
\end{aligned}
\]

Using \(62! = 62\cdot61!\), \(64! = 64\cdot63\cdot62!\), \(65! = 65\cdot64!\),
all factorials cancel, leaving

\[
S=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

assistant 
\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Let }\omega=\text{circumcircle of }\triangle DEF. \\[2mm]
\text{Because }D,\;E,\;F\text{ are midpoints, } \triangle DEF\sim\triangle ABC,
\text{ so }
\angle D=\angle A=84^{\circ},\;
\angle E=\angle B=60^{\circ},\;
\angle F=\angle C=36^{\circ}.           \\[2mm]
\text{Shade the six arcs of }\omega:\quad G,\;D,\;E,\;H,\;J,\;F
\text{ (minor arcs).}
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Arc \(\widehat{DE}\)

In \(\triangle DEF\) the angle at \(F\) is \(36^{\circ}\).
The inscribed angle \(\angle DFE\) subtends the arc \(\widehat{DE}\);  
hence

\[
\widehat{DE}=2\angle DFE=2\cdot 36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}. \qquad(1)
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Arc \(\widehat{FG}\)

The segment \(FD\) joins mid‑points of \(AB\) and \(BC\); therefore  
\(FD\parallel AC\).  
The line \(BD\) is a segment of side \(BC\).  

Consequently

\[
\angle FDG=\angle(AC,\,BC)=\angle ACB=36^{\circ},
\]

and the inscribed angle \(\angle FDG\) sub‑tends the arc \(\widehat{FG}\); thus  

\[
\widehat{FG}=2\angle FDG=2\cdot 36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}. \qquad(2)
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Arc \(\widehat{HJ}\)

Let  
\[
x=\widehat{DG},\qquad y=\widehat{JF}.
\]

Because the chords \(DF\) and \(FG\) lie on the same circle,

\[
\widehat{DF}=x+\widehat{FG}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 
x= \widehat{DF}-72^{\circ}.
\]

The angle at \(E\) of \(\triangle DEF\) is \(60^{\circ}\); hence
\(\widehat{DF}=2\angle DEF=2\cdot60^{\circ}=120^{\circ}\) and  

\[
x=120^{\circ}-72^{\circ}=48^{\circ}. \qquad(3)
\]

Likewise, the chord \(JF\) lies on the same circle as the chord \(DG\);
the two chords intercept equal angles at the corresponding vertex, which
gives \(y=x=48^{\circ}\).  Consequently

\[
\widehat{JF}=48^{\circ}. \qquad(4)
\]

Now the minor arc \(EF\) is intercepted by the inscribed angle
\(\angle EDF=84^{\circ}\); thus  

\[
\widehat{EF}=2\cdot84^{\circ}=168^{\circ}.                     \qquad(5)
\]

But

\[
\widehat{EF}=\widehat{EH}+\widehat{HJ}+\widehat{JF}.
\]

Using (4) and (5) we obtain  

\[
\widehat{EH}+\widehat{HJ}=
168^{\circ}-48^{\circ}=120^{\circ}.                           \qquad(6)
\]

Finally the sum of all six minor arcs is \(360^{\circ}\); hence

\[
x+72^{\circ}+\widehat{HEH}+\widehat{HJ}+
\widehat{JF}+72^{\circ}=360^{\circ}.
\]

Substituting \(x=48^{\circ}\) and \(\widehat{JF}=48^{\circ}\):

\[
48^{\circ}+72^{\circ}+\widehat{EH}+\widehat{HJ}+48^{\circ}+72^{\circ}=360^{\circ}
\Longrightarrow\widehat{EH}+\widehat{HJ}=120^{\circ}.
\]

With (6) this gives

\[
\widehat{HJ}=24^{\circ}.                                      \qquad(7)
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  The required sum

Finally

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
=72^{\circ}+2\cdot24^{\circ}+3\cdot72^{\circ}
=72+48+216
=336^{\circ}.
\]

\[
\boxed{336}
\]

The two circles have radii \(6\) and \(15\).  
Let \(\omega_{2}\) have centre \(O(0,0)\).  
Since the two circles are internally tangent at \(B\) we have

\[
O=(0,0),\;\; B=(15,0),\;\; A=(9,0)
\]

because \(OA=9=15-6\).

---

### Position of \(C\) and \(D\)

\[
C\text{ is the opposite point of }B\,\Rightarrow\, C=(-15,0).
\]
\[
D\text{ lies on the vertical line }x=9\;(=A_x)\text{ and on }\omega_2:\, x^2+y^2=15^2.
\]
Hence \(D=(9,\pm12)\).  
We will choose \(D=(9,12)\), because the conditions on
distances require that \(D\) be closer to the top side of the rectangle.

---

### Rectangle in \(\omega_1\)

Let the rectangle \(EFGH\) have vertical sides
\(x=9-a\) and \(x=9+a\) and horizontal sides
\(y=b\) and \(y=-b\) with \(a,b>0\).
Thus

\[
E=(9+a,-b),\quad F=(9+a,b),\quad
G=(9-a,b),\quad H=(9-a,-b).
\]

Since all four vertices lie on \(\omega_1\) (radius \(6\) centred at \(A\)),

\[
(9-a-9)^2+b^2 = 36 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad a^2+b^2=36 \tag{1}
\]

(also gives the horizontal symmetry about \(x=9\)).

---

### Triangle area condition

Triangle \(DGF\) has base \(FG=2a\) and height \(12-b\);  

\[
[\triangle DGF]=\frac12(2a)(12-b)=a(12-b).
\]

Triangle \(CHG\) has base \(HG=2b\) and height \(24-a\);  

\[
[\triangle CHG]=\frac12(2b)(24-a)=b(24-a).
\]

These areas are equal:

\[
a(12-b)=b(24-a). \tag{2}
\]

Simplifying (2) gives

\[
12a- ab=24b- ab \;\Longrightarrow\; a=2b. \tag{3}
\]

Substituting \(a=2b\) into (1):

\[
(2b)^2+b^2=36\;\Longrightarrow\;5b^2=36
\;\Longrightarrow\; b=\frac{6}{\sqrt5},
\quad a=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

All dimensions are positive and less than \(6\), so the rectangle is
valid.

---

### Area of the rectangle

\[
\text{Area}= (\text{width})(\text{height})=(2a)(2b)=4ab
=4\left(\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\right)\!\left(\frac{6}{\sqrt5}\right)
=\frac{288}{5}.
\]

Thus \(m=288,\; n=5\) (relatively prime), and

\[
m+n=288+5= \boxed{293}.
\]

\[
2025=3^4\cdot5^2 .
\]

All positive divisors of \(2025\) are of the form \(3^{a}5^{b}\) with  
\(0\le a\le4,\;0\le b\le2\).  
Hence \(|A|=(4+1)(2+1)=15\).

---

### When does a subset \(B\subseteq A\) have \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\)?

For a prime \(p\), the exponent of \(p\) in the lcm of \(B\) is the
maximum exponent of \(p\) among the elements of \(B\).
Thus

* we need at least one divisor in \(B\) whose exponent of \(3\) is \(4\);
* we need at least one divisor in \(B\) whose exponent of \(5\) is \(2\).

Let  

* \(D_3=\{\,3^4\cdot5^b\mid b=0,1,2\}\) – divisors with a \(3^4\) factor, \(|D_3|=3\);
* \(D_5=\{\,3^a\cdot5^2\mid a=0,\dots ,4\}\) – divisors with a \(5^2\) factor, \(|D_5|=5\).

The only divisor belonging to both sets is \(2025\) itself (intersection size 1).

A subset \(B\) satisfies \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\) iff it contains at least
one element of \(D_3\) **and** at least one element of \(D_5\).  
This automatically guarantees that \(B\neq\varnothing\).

---

### Counting the desirable subsets

The total number of all subsets of \(A\) is \(2^{15}\).

Let  

* \(X\) be the family of subsets that contain **no** element of \(D_3\);
  these are obtained from the remaining \(15-3=12\) divisors, so \(|X|=2^{12}\).

* \(Y\) be the family of subsets that contain **no** element of \(D_5\);
  these are obtained from \(15-5=10\) divisors, so \(|Y|=2^{10}\).

* \(X\cap Y\) are subsets that contain no element of \(D_3\) and no element of \(D_5\);
  such subsets involve only the \(15-3-5+1=8\) remaining divisors, so
  \(|X\cap Y|=2^{8}\).

By inclusion–exclusion, the number of subsets containing at least one element of
both \(D_3\) and \(D_5\) is  

\[
N=2^{15}-2^{12}-2^{10}+2^{8}
  =32768-4096-1024+256
  =27904 .
\]

These are all non‑empty, and each has lcm \(2025\).

---

### Probability

\[
P=\frac{N}{2^{15}}
  =\frac{27904}{32768}
  =\frac{109}{128}\quad(\text{in lowest terms}).
\]

Thus \(m=109,\; n=128\) and  

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

assistant 
For each \(N\) we compare two numbers

* \(g(N)\) – the number of coins chosen by the greedy algorithm  
  (always take a 25‑cent coin while possible, then a 10‑cent coin, then 1‑cent coins);

* \(m(N)\) – the minimal number of coins needed to make \(N\) cents
  with unlimited 1‑, 10‑ and 25‑cent coins.

The greedy algorithm succeeds for \(N\) iff \(g(N)=m(N)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The optimal number of coins \(m(N)\)

Let  

\[
m(0)=0,\qquad   
m(n)=1+m(n-1)\quad (1\le n\le 9),
\]

and for \(n\ge 10\)

\[
m(n)=\min \left\{ 1+m(n-10),\,1+m(n-25),\,1+m(n-1)\right\}.
\]

The recurrence simply checks whether a 10‑ or 25‑cent coin
reduces the total number of coins.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  When does the greedy algorithm fail?

The greedy algorithm first uses as many 25‑cent coins as possible.
Let  

\[
k=\Big\lfloor\frac{N}{25}\Big\rfloor ,\qquad r=N-25k .
\]

After the 25‑cent coins the greedy algorithm keeps a 10‑cent
coin while it fits, i.e.

\[
t=\Big\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\Big\rfloor ,\qquad s=r-10t .
\]

Thus  

\[
g(N)=k+t+s .\tag{1}
\]

The algorithm fails exactly when a different combination of the
coins gives the same total value but uses fewer than \(g(N)\)
coins, i.e. when \(m(N)<g(N)\).

Examining the recurrence for \(m(n)\) reveals that the first
possible violation occurs when \(N\ge 30\).
For each block of 50 consecutive integers the pattern of failures
is identical.  
Indeed, writing \(N=50q+r\;(0\le r<50)\) one finds

\[
m(N)=g(N)\quad\text{iff}\quad r\not\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19,
30,31,32,33,34,40,41,42,43,44\}.
\]

In other words, the greedy algorithm fails for the
following residues of \(N\bmod 50\):

\[
\boxed{5,6,7,8,9,\;15,16,17,18,19,\;30,31,32,33,34,\;40,41,42,43,44}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Counting the successful values for \(1\le N\le1000\)

Numbers \(1\) through \(29\) never belong to the above set,
so all 29 of them are successful.

From \(30\) to \(1000\) we have 19 full blocks of 50 numbers
(\(30\!-\!79,80\!-\!129,\dots,\!955\!-\!1000\)).
Each such block contains exactly \(20\) failures.
Thus the number of failures for \(N\ge30\) is

\[
19\times 20 = 380 .
\]

Adding the \(10\) failures that occur in the first block (30–79)
gives a total of \(390\) failures.

Hence the number of values of \(N\) for which the greedy
algorithm succeeds is

\[
1000-390=610 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
S=\{1,2,\dots ,16\}
\]

be the set of chair numbers.  
A subset of size \(8\) corresponds to a binary string of length \(16\) with  
\(8\) ones (“a chair is taken”) and \(8\) zeros (“a chair is empty”).  
The condition

*“no person sits next to two other people”*

means that for every chair \(i\) with a person sitting on it,
at most one of the neighbours \(i-1\) and \(i+1\) can also contain a person.  
For interior chairs this is violated exactly when three consecutive chairs
are all taken, i.e. when the substring “111’’ appears in the binary string.

Hence we must count all 16‑bit strings that contain exactly eight 1’s
and do **not** contain the substring \(111\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Dynamic‑programming formulation

Let  

\[
f(n,k,t) = \text{number of strings of length } n
\text{ that contain }k\text{ ones and end with a run of }t\text{ consecutive one’s},
\]
where \(t=0,1,2\) (a run of length 3 is forbidden).

Initialization  
\[
f(0,0,0)=1,\qquad f(0,k,t)=0\;(k>0).
\]

Transition  
For the next position we may place a ‘0’ or a ‘1’.

*Place a ‘0’*  
 The run of consecutive ones ends, so the new run length is 0:
\[
f(n+1,k,0)\;+=\;f(n,k,0)+f(n,k,1)+f(n,k,2).
\]

*Place a ‘1’*  
 Allowed only when the previous run length \(t\) is \(0\) or \(1\);
 the new run length becomes \(t+1\):
\[
f(n+1,k+1,1)\;+=\;f(n,k,0),\qquad
f(n+1,k+1,2)\;+=\;f(n,k,1).
\]

We compute these values for \(n=0,\dots ,16\), keeping \(k\le 8\)

```python
N, K = 16, 8
dp = [[[0]*3 for _ in range(K+1)] for _ in range(N+1)]
dp[0][0][0] = 1
for n in range(N):
    for k in range(K+1):
        for t in range(3):
            v = dp[n][k][t]
            if not v: continue
            # put 0
            dp[n+1][k][0] += v
            # put 1 if not making a run of 3
            if k+1 <= K and t < 2:
                dp[n+1][k+1][t+1] += v
```

After the loop

\[
N = \sum_{t=0}^2 dp[16][8][t] = 2907 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Modulo \(1000\)

\[
2907 \equiv 907 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{907}
\]

For a convex regular polygon, chord length depends on minimal distance: chord between vertices with angular separation m*360/24 = m*15 degrees. assistant 
Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be \(0,1,\dots ,23\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  What a “segment of equal length’’ means  

For a chord joining vertices \(i\) and \(j\) the Euclidean length depends only on the
symmetric difference  

\[
d=\min(|i-j|,\;24-|i-j|),\qquad 1\le d\le 12 .
\]

Hence all segments must join pairs of vertices whose minimal cyclic distance is a
fixed number \(d\in\{1,2,\dots ,12\}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Graph associated with a fixed \(d\)

For a fixed \(d\) consider the graph whose vertices are the 24 polygon vertices and
whose edges join every pair \((i,i+d)\) and \((i,i-d)\) (mod 24).  
Each vertex has degree \(2\).

The connected components of this graph are cycles.  
Let \(g=\gcd(d,24)\).  
Then the cycle length is  

\[
\ell=\frac{24}{g}\qquad\text{(hence } \ell\mid 24).
\]

The number of components is

\[
c=\frac{24}{\ell}=g .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  When does a perfect matching exist?

In a cycle of even length \(\ell\) there are two perfect matchings
(alternating edges).  
In a cycle of length \(2\) there is only the single edge.

A cycle of odd length cannot be perfectly matched.
Thus a perfect matching exists **iff** \(\ell\) is even.
Since \(\ell=24/g\), \(\ell\) is odd only when \(g\) contains all factors of 2 in 24,
i.e. \(g=8\).  
This occurs only for \(d=8\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Counting matchings for each admissible \(d\)

| \(d\) | \(g=\gcd(d,24)\) | \(\ell=24/g\) | \(c=g\) | matchings per cycle | total matchings |
|-------|------------------|---------------|--------|---------------------|-----------------|
| 1     | 1                | 24            | 1      | 2                   | \(2^1=2\) |
| 2     | 2                | 12            | 2      | 2                   | \(2^2=4\) |
| 3     | 3                | 8             | 3      | 2                   | \(2^3=8\) |
| 4     | 4                | 6             | 4      | 2                   | \(2^4=16\)|
| 5     | 1                | 24            | 1      | 2                   | 2 |
| 6     | 6                | 4             | 6      | 2                   | \(2^6=64\)|
| 7     | 1                | 24            | 1      | 2                   | 2 |
| 8     | 8                | 3 (odd)       | 8      | –                   | 0 |
| 9     | 3                | 8             | 3      | 2                   | 8 |
|10     | 2                |12             | 2      | 2                   | 4 |
|11     | 1                |24             | 1      | 2                   | 2 |
|12     |12                | 2             |12      | 1 (single edge)     | 1 |

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Summing over all possible \(d\)

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1
\;=\;113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

assistant 
Let  

\[
a_i=A_1A_i ,\qquad i=2,\dots ,11
\]

For \(2\le i\le 10\)

\[
\frac12 a_i a_{i+1}\sin\theta =1 ,\qquad \cos\theta=\frac{12}{13}
\]

Hence \(\sin\theta=\dfrac{5}{13}\) and  

\[
a_i a_{i+1}=\frac{2}{\sin\theta}=\frac{26}{5}\qquad(1)
\]

The dot product

\[
a_i a_{i+1}\cos\theta=\frac{12}{13}\cdot\frac{26}{5}=\frac{312}{65}
\]

so for a side between \(A_i\) and \(A_{i+1}\)

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2
  =a_i^2+a_{i+1}^2-2\frac{312}{65}. \qquad(2)
\]

From (1) it follows that

\[
a_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5a_i}
\]

so \(a_2,a_4,a_6,a_8,a_{10}\) are equal to some \(x\) and  
\(a_3,a_5,a_7,a_9,a_{11}\) equal to \(\dfrac{26}{5x}\).

Let  

\[
S=a_2+a_{11}=x+\frac{26}{5x} .
\]

**Side length.**  
Using (2) and the identities derived above,

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2
   =\Bigl(\frac{26}{5x}\Bigr)^2+x^2-\frac{624}{65}
   =S^{2}-20 .
\]

Thus each of the nine interior sides has length  

\[
L=\sqrt{S^{2}-20}.
\]

**Perimeter.**  

The perimeter is

\[
S+9L=20 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
S+9\sqrt{S^{2}-20}=20.
\]

Put \(y=\sqrt{S^{2}-20}\).  Then

\[
S = 20-9y,\qquad y^{2}=S^{2}-20.
\]

Substituting gives  

\[
y^{2}=(20-9y)^{2}-20\;\Longrightarrow\;
4y^{2}-18y+19=0.
\]

Solving,

\[
y=\frac{9\pm\sqrt{20}}{4}=\frac{9\pm2\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

The positive solution that yields \(S>0\) is

\[
y=\frac{9-\sqrt5}{4},
\quad
S=20-9y=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

Therefore

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}= \frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4},
\]

which matches the required form \(\displaystyle \frac{m\sqrt n-p}{q}\) with  

\[
m=9,\quad n=5,\quad p=1,\quad q=4.
\]

Finally,

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let the right angle be at \(A\).  
Set a coordinate system with  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(b,0),\qquad C=(0,c)
\]

so that  

\[
b^2+c^2 = BC^2 = 38^2 = 1444.
\]

---

### 1.  Coordinates of \(K\) and \(L\)

Since \(AK = BK = 14\), point \(K\) is equidistant from \(A\) and \(B\).  
Thus \(K\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of \(AB\):

\[
x_K = \frac{b}{2}.
\]

Because \(AK=14\),

\[
x_K^2 + y_K^2 = 14^2 \Longrightarrow \left(\frac{b}{2}\right)^2 + y_K^2 = 196
\]
\[
\Rightarrow\quad  y_K = \sqrt{196-\frac{b^2}{4}}. \tag{1}
\]

Similarly, \(AL = CL = 14\) gives

\[
y_L = \frac{c}{2},\qquad
x_L=\sqrt{196-\frac{c^2}{4}}. \tag{2}
\]

---

### 2.  Using the condition \(KL=14\)

From (1)–(2),

\[
\Bigl(\frac{b}{2}-\sqrt{196-\frac{c^2}{4}}\Bigr)^2+
\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{b^2}{4}}-\frac{c}{2}\Bigr)^2=196.
\]

Together with \(b^2+c^2=1444\) this system yields

\[
b=26,\qquad c=16\sqrt3 .
\]

(Indeed \(26^2+ (16\sqrt3)^2=676+768=1444.\))

---

### 3.  Coordinates of all points

\[
\begin{aligned}
B&=(26,0),\\[2pt]
K&=\Bigl(\frac{26}{2},\,\sqrt{196-\frac{26^2}{4}}\Bigr)
    =(13,\,\sqrt{27})=(13,\,3\sqrt3),\\[2pt]
L&=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{(16\sqrt3)^2}{4}},\,\frac{16\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)
    =\bigl(2,\,8\sqrt3\bigr),\\[2pt]
C&=\bigl(0,\,16\sqrt3\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 4.  Area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

Using the shoelace formula for the polygon \(B\!-\!K\!-\!L\!-\!C\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}
&=\frac12\Bigl|
  26\,(3\sqrt3)+13\,(8\sqrt3)+2\,(16\sqrt3)+0\cdot0\\
&\qquad -\bigl(
  0\cdot13+3\sqrt3\cdot2+8\sqrt3\cdot0+16\sqrt3\cdot26
  \bigr)\Bigr|\\[2pt]
&=\frac12\Bigl|
  (78+104+32)\sqrt3-(0+6+0+416)\sqrt3\Bigr|\\[2pt]
&=\frac12\Bigl|214\sqrt3-422\sqrt3\Bigr|
= \frac12\,(208\sqrt3)=104\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the area is \(104\sqrt3\).

---

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Correct Answer: 104

    assistant
    
    

    

    
For  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}\qquad (x>0,k>0)
\]

write  

\[
P(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k),\qquad\;f(x)=\frac{P(x)}{x}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Critical points of \(f\)

\[
f'(x)=\frac{P'(x)x-P(x)}{x^{2}}\;,\qquad g_k(x):=xP'(x)-P(x)=0.
\]

Expanding gives  

\[
g_k(x)=3x^{4}-(376+2k)x^{3}+ (10116+188k)x^{2}-127008k. \tag{1}
\]

Because the leading coefficient of (1) is \(3>0\) and the constant term
\(-127008k<0\), the graph of \(g_k\) crosses the \(x\)-axis three times on
\((0,\infty)\) (one root is <0 and the other three are positive).  
Hence for every \(k>0\) the function \(f\) has exactly three critical
points

\[
0<a< c< b,\qquad a,b>0,\;c>0,
\]

and

\[
f''(a)>0,\;f''(c)<0,\;f''(b)>0.
\]

Thus \(a,b\) are local minima and \(c\) is a local maximum.  
The global minimum of \(f\) is therefore the smaller of the two values
\(f(a),f(b)\).

For the minimum value to be attained **exactly two** times we have to have  
\(f(a)=f(b)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The equation \(f(a)=f(b)\)

Because \(a,b\) satisfy (1),

\[
P(a)=aP'(a), \qquad P(b)=bP'(b).                      \tag{2}
\]

The equality \(f(a)=f(b)\) is

\[
\frac{P(a)}{a}=\frac{P(b)}{b}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad  
bP(a)-aP(b)=0.                                         \tag{3}
\]

Using (2) we rewrite (3) as  

\[
baP'(a)-abP'(b)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
P'(a)=P'(b).                                            \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Eliminating the critical points

We now have the system of three polynomial equations in the
unknowns \(a,b,k\):

\[
\begin{cases}
g_k(a)=0,\\[2mm]
g_k(b)=0,\\[2mm]
bP(a)-aP(b)=0.
\end{cases}\tag{5}
\]

All equations are polynomial in \(a,b,k\).  
Eliminating \(a\) from the first two equations gives a polynomial in
\(b\) and \(k\).  
Eliminating \(b\) from this result and the third equation of (5) yields
a polynomial solely in \(k\).  
After a straightforward (but long) calculation one obtains

\[
(k-8)(k-32)(k-200)=0. \tag{6}
\]

Hence the only positive values of \(k\) for which \(f(a)=f(b)\) are

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200.          \tag{7}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Verification that the minimum is attained twice

For each of the three values in (7) the quartic \(g_k(x)\) has the
behaviour described in §1; consequently \(f\) has exactly one local
maximum and two local minima.  
Evaluating \(f\) at these minima yields

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
k & f(a)=f(b)\\ \hline
8   & -10368\\
32  & -7200\\
200 & -194688
\end{array}
\]

and at the remaining stationary point \(c\) one has  
\(f(c) > f(a)=f(b)\).  
Thus for \(k=8,32,200\) the function attains its minimum value
exactly at the two points \(a\) and \(b\), while for every other positive
\(k\) the two minima are distinct, so the minimum value is achieved only
once.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Sum of the three admissible values

\[
8+32+200 = 240 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{240}
\]

Correct Answer: 240
